{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /logs/agent/sessions/skills/discover-important-function\n\n# Fuzz Target Localizer\n\n## Purpose\n\nThis skill helps an agent quickly narrow a Python repository down to a small set of high-value fuzz targets. It produces:\n\n- A ranked list of important files\n- A ranked list of important functions and methods\n- A summary of existing unit tests and inferred oracles\n- A final shortlist of functions-under-test (FUTs) for fuzzing, each with a structured \"note to self\" for follow-up actions\n\n## When to use\n\nUse this skill when the user asks to:\n\n- Find the best functions to fuzz in a Python package/library\n- Identify parsers, decoders, validators, or boundary code that is fuzz-worthy\n- Decide what to fuzz based on existing test coverage and API surface\n- Produce a structured shortlist of fuzz targets with harness guidance\n- Automating testing pipeline setup for a new or existing Python project.\n\nDo not use this skill when the user primarily wants to fix a specific bug,\nrefactor code, or implement a harness immediately\n(unless they explicitly ask for target selection first).\n\n## Inputs expected from the environment\n\nThe agent should assume access to:\n\n- Repository filesystem\n- Ability to read files\n- Ability to run local commands (optional but recommended)\n\nPreferred repository listing command:\n\n- Use `tree` to get a fast, high-signal view of repository structure (limit depth if needed).\n\nIf `tree` is not available, fall back to a recursive listing via other standard shell tooling.\n\n## Outputs\n\nProduce result:\n\nA report in any format with the following information: - Localize important files - Localize important functions - Summarize existing unit tests - Decide function under test for fuzzing\nThis file should be placed in the root of the repository as `APIs.txt`,\nwhich servers as the guidelines for future fuzzing harness implementation.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Prefer analysis and reporting over code changes.\n- Do not modify source code unless the user explicitly requests changes.\n- If running commands could be disruptive, default to read-only analysis.\n- Avoid assumptions about runtime behavior; base conclusions on code and tests.\n- Explicitly consider existing tests in the repo when selecting targets and deciding harness shape.\n- Keep the final FUT shortlist small (typically 1–5).\n\n---\n\n# Localize important files\n\n## Goal\n\nProduce a ranked list of files that are most likely to contain fuzz-worthy logic: parsing, decoding, deserialization, validation, protocol handling, file/network boundaries, or native bindings.\n\n## Procedure\n\n1. Build a repository map\n    - Start with a repository overview using `tree` to identify:\n        - Root packages and layouts (src/ layout vs flat layout)\n        - Test directories and configs\n        - Bindings/native directories\n        - Examples, docs, and tooling directories\n    - Identify packaging and metadata files such as:\n        - pyproject.toml\n        - setup.cfg\n        - setup.py\n    - Identify test configuration and entry points:\n        - tests/\n        - conftest.py\n        - pytest.ini\n        - tox.ini\n        - noxfile.py\n\n2. Exclude low-value areas\n    - Skip: virtual environments, build outputs, vendored code, documentation-only directories, examples-only directories, generated files.\n\n3. Score files using explainable heuristics\n   Assign a file a higher score when it matches more of these indicators:\n    - Public API exposure: **init**.py re-exports, **all**, api modules\n    - Input boundary keywords in file path or symbols: parse, load, dump, decode, encode, deserialize, serialize, validate, normalize, schema, protocol, message\n    - Format handlers: json, yaml, xml, csv, toml, protobuf, msgpack, pickle\n    - Regex-heavy or templating-heavy code\n    - Native boundaries: ctypes, cffi, cython, extension modules, bindings\n    - Central modules: high import fan-in across the package\n    - Test adjacency: directly imported or heavily referenced by tests\n\n4. Rank and select\n    - Produce a Top-N list (default 10–30) with a short rationale per file.\n\n## Output format\n\nFor each file:\n\n- path\n- score (relative, not necessarily normalized)\n- rationale (2–5 bullets)\n- indicators_hit (list)\n\n---\n\n# Localize important functions\n\n## Goal\n\nFrom the important files, identify and rank functions or methods that are strong fuzz targets while minimizing full-body reading until needed.\n\n## Approach 1: AST-based header and docstring scan\n\nFor each localized Python file, parse it using Python’s `ast` module and extract only:\n\n- Module docstring\n- Function and async function headers (name, args, defaults, annotations, decorators)\n- Class headers and method headers\n- Docstrings for modules, classes, and functions/methods\n\nDo not read full function bodies during the initial pass unless needed for disambiguation or final selection.\n\n### Procedure\n\n1. Build per-file declarations via AST\n    - Parse the file with `ast`\n    - Enumerate:\n        - Top-level functions\n        - Classes and their methods\n        - Nested functions only if they are likely to be directly fuzzable via an exposed wrapper\n    - For each symbol, collect:\n        - Fully-qualified name\n        - Signature details (as available from AST)\n        - Decorators\n        - Docstring (if present)\n        - Location information (file, line range if available)\n\n2. Generate an initial candidate set using headers and docstrings\n   Prioritize functions/methods that:\n    - Accept bytes, str, file-like objects, dicts, or user-controlled payloads\n    - Convert between representations (raw ↔ structured)\n    - Perform validation, normalization, parsing, decoding, deserialization\n    - Touch filesystem/network/protocol boundaries\n    - Call into native extensions or bindings\n    - Clearly document strictness, schemas, formats, or error conditions\n\n3. Use tests to refine candidate selection early\n    - Before reading full bodies, check if tests reference these functions/modules:\n        - Direct imports in tests\n        - Fixtures that exercise particular entry points\n        - Parameterizations over formats and inputs\n    - Down-rank candidates that are already well-covered unless they are high-risk boundaries (parsers/native bindings).\n\n4. Confirm with targeted reading only for top candidates\n   For the top candidates (typically 10–20), read the full function bodies and capture:\n    - Preconditions and assumptions\n    - Internal helpers called\n    - Error handling style and exception types\n    - Any obvious invariants and postconditions\n    - Statefulness and global dependencies\n\n5. Rank and shortlist\n   Rank candidates using an explainable rubric:\n    - Input surface and reachability\n    - Boundary risk (parsing/decoding/native)\n    - Structural complexity (from targeted reading only)\n    - Existing test coverage strength and breadth\n    - Ease of harnessing\n\n### Output format\n\nFor each function/method:\n\n- qualname\n- file\n- line_range (if available)\n- score (relative)\n- rationale (2–6 bullets)\n- dependencies (key helpers, modules, external state)\n- harnessability (low/medium/high)\n\n## Approach 2: Scanning all important files yourself\n\nFor each localized Python file, read the file contents directly to extract:\n\n- Module docstring and overall structure\n- Function and async function definitions (name, args, defaults, annotations, decorators)\n- Class definitions and their methods\n- Full function bodies and implementation details\n- Docstrings for modules, classes, and functions/methods\n\nThis approach reads complete file contents, allowing for deeper analysis at the cost of higher token usage.\n\n### Procedure\n\n1. Read important files sequentially\n    - For each file from the important files list, read the full contents.\n    - Extract by direct inspection:\n        - Top-level functions and their complete implementations\n        - Classes and their methods with full bodies\n        - Nested functions if they are exposed or called by public APIs\n    - For each symbol, collect:\n        - Fully-qualified name\n        - Complete signature (from source text)\n        - Decorators\n        - Docstring (if present)\n        - Full function body\n        - Location information (file, approximate line range)\n\n2. Generate an initial candidate set using full source analysis\n   Prioritize functions/methods that:\n    - Accept bytes, str, file-like objects, dicts, or user-controlled payloads\n    - Convert between representations (raw ↔ structured)\n    - Perform validation, normalization, parsing, decoding, deserialization\n    - Touch filesystem/network/protocol boundaries\n    - Call into native extensions or bindings\n    - Contain complex control flow, loops, or recursive calls\n    - Handle exceptions or edge cases\n    - Clearly document strictness, schemas, formats, or error conditions\n\n3. Analyze implementation details from full bodies\n   For each candidate function, inspect the body for:\n    - Preconditions and assumptions (explicit checks, assertions, early returns)\n    - Internal helpers called and their purposes\n    - Error handling style and exception types raised\n    - Invariants and postconditions (explicit or implicit)\n    - Statefulness and global dependencies\n    - Input transformations and data flow\n    - Native calls or external process invocations\n    - Resource allocation and cleanup patterns\n\n4. Use tests to refine candidate selection\n    - Check if tests reference these functions/modules:\n        - Direct imports in tests\n        - Fixtures that exercise particular entry points\n        - Parameterizations over formats and inputs\n    - Down-rank candidates that are already well-covered unless they are high-risk boundaries (parsers/native bindings).\n    - Note which aspects of each function are tested vs untested.\n\n5. Rank and shortlist\n   Rank candidates using an explainable rubric:\n    - Input surface and reachability\n    - Boundary risk (parsing/decoding/native)\n    - Structural complexity (from full body analysis)\n    - Existing test coverage strength and breadth\n    - Ease of harnessing\n    - Observable implementation risks (unsafe operations, unchecked inputs, complex state)\n\n### Output format\n\nFor each function/method:\n\n- qualname\n- file\n- line_range (if available)\n- score (relative)\n- rationale (2–6 bullets)\n- dependencies (key helpers, modules, external state)\n- harnessability (low/medium/high)\n- implementation_notes (key observations from body analysis)\n\n---\n\n# Summarize existing unit tests\n\n## Goal\n\nSummarize what is already tested, infer test oracles, and identify gaps that fuzzing can complement.\n\n## Hard requirement\n\nAlways inspect and incorporate existing tests in the repository when:\n\n- Ranking functions\n- Selecting FUTs\n- Designing input models and oracles\n- Proposing seed corpus sources\n\n## Procedure\n\n1. Inventory tests\n    - Locate tests and their discovery configuration (pytest.ini, pyproject.toml, tox.ini, noxfile.py).\n    - Enumerate test modules and map them to source modules via imports.\n    - Identify shared fixtures and data factories (conftest.py, fixture files, test utilities).\n\n2. Summarize test intent\n   For each test module:\n    - What behaviors are asserted\n    - What inputs are used\n    - What exceptions are expected\n    - What invariants are implied\n\n3. Infer oracles and properties\n   Common fuzz-friendly oracles include:\n    - Round-trip properties\n    - Idempotence of normalization\n    - Parser consistency across equivalent inputs\n    - Deterministic output given deterministic input\n    - No-crash and no-hang for malformed inputs\n\n4. Identify coverage gaps\n    - FUT candidates with no direct tests\n    - Input classes not covered by tests (size extremes, malformed encodings, deep nesting, edge unicode, invalid schemas)\n    - Code paths guarded by complex conditionals or exception handlers with no tests\n\n## Output format\n\n- test_map: module_under_test → tests → asserted behaviors\n- inferred_oracles: list of reusable invariants with the functions they apply to\n- gaps: ranked list of untested or weakly-tested candidates\n\n---\n\n# Decide function under test for fuzzing\n\n## Goal\n\nSelect a final set of FUTs (typically 1–5) and produce a structured \"note to self\" for each FUT so the agent can proceed to harness implementation, corpus seeding, and fuzz execution.\n\n## Selection criteria\n\nPrefer FUTs that maximize:\n\n- Security/robustness payoff (parsing, decoding, validation, native boundary)\n- Reachability with minimal setup\n- Low existing test assurance or narrow test input coverage\n- High fuzzability (simple input channel, clear oracle or crash-only target)\n\nExplicitly weigh:\n\n- What tests already cover (and what they do not)\n- What seeds can be extracted from tests, fixtures, and sample data\n\n## Required \"note to self\" template\n\nFor each selected FUT, produce exactly this structure:\n\nFuzzing Target Note\n\n- Target:\n- File / location:\n- Why this target:\n    - Input surface:\n    - Boundary or native considerations:\n    - Complexity or path depth:\n    - Current test gaps:\n- Callable contract:\n    - Required imports or initialization:\n    - Preconditions:\n    - Determinism concerns:\n    - External dependencies:\n- Input model:\n    - Primary payload type:\n    - Decoding or parsing steps:\n    - Constraints to respect:\n    - Edge classes to emphasize:\n- Oracles:\n    - Must-hold properties:\n    - Acceptable exceptions:\n    - Suspicious exceptions:\n    - Crash-only vs correctness-checking:\n- Harness plan:\n    - Recommended approach:\n    - Minimal harness signature:\n    - Seed corpus ideas:\n    - Timeouts and resource limits:\n- Risk flags:\n    - Native extension involved:\n    - Potential DoS paths:\n    - External I/O:\n- Next actions:\n    1.\n    2.\n    3.\n    4.\n\n## Output format\n\n- selected_futs: list of chosen FUTs with brief justification\n- notes_to_self: one \"Fuzzing Target Note\" per FUT\n\n---\n\n# Final JSON block\n\nAt the end of the report, include a JSON object with:\n\n- important_files: [{path, score, rationale, indicators_hit}]\n- important_functions: [{qualname, file, line_range, score, rationale, dependencies, harnessability}]\n- test_summary: {test_map, inferred_oracles, gaps}\n- selected_futs: [{qualname, file, line_range, justification}]\n- notes_to_self: [{target_qualname, note}]\n\n\nARGUMENTS: /app/arrow"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"56e145a4-0d6b-4a75-be1f-7887ea2c2e6e","uuid":"7c48360a-4c4e-4d80-a6bd-69128b89243f","isSynthetic":true}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /logs/agent/sessions/skills/discover-important-function\n\n# Fuzz Target Localizer\n\n## Purpose\n\nThis skill helps an agent quickly narrow a Python repository down to a small set of high-value fuzz targets. It produces:\n\n- A ranked list of important files\n- A ranked list of important functions and methods\n- A summary of existing unit tests and inferred oracles\n- A final shortlist of functions-under-test (FUTs) for fuzzing, each with a structured \"note to self\" for follow-up actions\n\n## When to use\n\nUse this skill when the user asks to:\n\n- Find the best functions to fuzz in a Python package/library\n- Identify parsers, decoders, validators, or boundary code that is fuzz-worthy\n- Decide what to fuzz based on existing test coverage and API surface\n- Produce a structured shortlist of fuzz targets with harness guidance\n- Automating testing pipeline setup for a new or existing Python project.\n\nDo not use this skill when the user primarily wants to fix a specific bug,\nrefactor code, or implement a harness immediately\n(unless they explicitly ask for target selection first).\n\n## Inputs expected from the environment\n\nThe agent should assume access to:\n\n- Repository filesystem\n- Ability to read files\n- Ability to run local commands (optional but recommended)\n\nPreferred repository listing command:\n\n- Use `tree` to get a fast, high-signal view of repository structure (limit depth if needed).\n\nIf `tree` is not available, fall back to a recursive listing via other standard shell tooling.\n\n## Outputs\n\nProduce result:\n\nA report in any format with the following information: - Localize important files - Localize important functions - Summarize existing unit tests - Decide function under test for fuzzing\nThis file should be placed in the root of the repository as `APIs.txt`,\nwhich servers as the guidelines for future fuzzing harness implementation.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Prefer analysis and reporting over code changes.\n- Do not modify source code unless the user explicitly requests changes.\n- If running commands could be disruptive, default to read-only analysis.\n- Avoid assumptions about runtime behavior; base conclusions on code and tests.\n- Explicitly consider existing tests in the repo when selecting targets and deciding harness shape.\n- Keep the final FUT shortlist small (typically 1–5).\n\n---\n\n# Localize important files\n\n## Goal\n\nProduce a ranked list of files that are most likely to contain fuzz-worthy logic: parsing, decoding, deserialization, validation, protocol handling, file/network boundaries, or native bindings.\n\n## Procedure\n\n1. Build a repository map\n    - Start with a repository overview using `tree` to identify:\n        - Root packages and layouts (src/ layout vs flat layout)\n        - Test directories and configs\n        - Bindings/native directories\n        - Examples, docs, and tooling directories\n    - Identify packaging and metadata files such as:\n        - pyproject.toml\n        - setup.cfg\n        - setup.py\n    - Identify test configuration and entry points:\n        - tests/\n        - conftest.py\n        - pytest.ini\n        - tox.ini\n        - noxfile.py\n\n2. Exclude low-value areas\n    - Skip: virtual environments, build outputs, vendored code, documentation-only directories, examples-only directories, generated files.\n\n3. Score files using explainable heuristics\n   Assign a file a higher score when it matches more of these indicators:\n    - Public API exposure: **init**.py re-exports, **all**, api modules\n    - Input boundary keywords in file path or symbols: parse, load, dump, decode, encode, deserialize, serialize, validate, normalize, schema, protocol, message\n    - Format handlers: json, yaml, xml, csv, toml, protobuf, msgpack, pickle\n    - Regex-heavy or templating-heavy code\n    - Native boundaries: ctypes, cffi, cython, extension modules, bindings\n    - Central modules: high import fan-in across the package\n    - Test adjacency: directly imported or heavily referenced by tests\n\n4. Rank and select\n    - Produce a Top-N list (default 10–30) with a short rationale per file.\n\n## Output format\n\nFor each file:\n\n- path\n- score (relative, not necessarily normalized)\n- rationale (2–5 bullets)\n- indicators_hit (list)\n\n---\n\n# Localize important functions\n\n## Goal\n\nFrom the important files, identify and rank functions or methods that are strong fuzz targets while minimizing full-body reading until needed.\n\n## Approach 1: AST-based header and docstring scan\n\nFor each localized Python file, parse it using Python’s `ast` module and extract only:\n\n- Module docstring\n- Function and async function headers (name, args, defaults, annotations, decorators)\n- Class headers and method headers\n- Docstrings for modules, classes, and functions/methods\n\nDo not read full function bodies during the initial pass unless needed for disambiguation or final selection.\n\n### Procedure\n\n1. Build per-file declarations via AST\n    - Parse the file with `ast`\n    - Enumerate:\n        - Top-level functions\n        - Classes and their methods\n        - Nested functions only if they are likely to be directly fuzzable via an exposed wrapper\n    - For each symbol, collect:\n        - Fully-qualified name\n        - Signature details (as available from AST)\n        - Decorators\n        - Docstring (if present)\n        - Location information (file, line range if available)\n\n2. Generate an initial candidate set using headers and docstrings\n   Prioritize functions/methods that:\n    - Accept bytes, str, file-like objects, dicts, or user-controlled payloads\n    - Convert between representations (raw ↔ structured)\n    - Perform validation, normalization, parsing, decoding, deserialization\n    - Touch filesystem/network/protocol boundaries\n    - Call into native extensions or bindings\n    - Clearly document strictness, schemas, formats, or error conditions\n\n3. Use tests to refine candidate selection early\n    - Before reading full bodies, check if tests reference these functions/modules:\n        - Direct imports in tests\n        - Fixtures that exercise particular entry points\n        - Parameterizations over formats and inputs\n    - Down-rank candidates that are already well-covered unless they are high-risk boundaries (parsers/native bindings).\n\n4. Confirm with targeted reading only for top candidates\n   For the top candidates (typically 10–20), read the full function bodies and capture:\n    - Preconditions and assumptions\n    - Internal helpers called\n    - Error handling style and exception types\n    - Any obvious invariants and postconditions\n    - Statefulness and global dependencies\n\n5. Rank and shortlist\n   Rank candidates using an explainable rubric:\n    - Input surface and reachability\n    - Boundary risk (parsing/decoding/native)\n    - Structural complexity (from targeted reading only)\n    - Existing test coverage strength and breadth\n    - Ease of harnessing\n\n### Output format\n\nFor each function/method:\n\n- qualname\n- file\n- line_range (if available)\n- score (relative)\n- rationale (2–6 bullets)\n- dependencies (key helpers, modules, external state)\n- harnessability (low/medium/high)\n\n## Approach 2: Scanning all important files yourself\n\nFor each localized Python file, read the file contents directly to extract:\n\n- Module docstring and overall structure\n- Function and async function definitions (name, args, defaults, annotations, decorators)\n- Class definitions and their methods\n- Full function bodies and implementation details\n- Docstrings for modules, classes, and functions/methods\n\nThis approach reads complete file contents, allowing for deeper analysis at the cost of higher token usage.\n\n### Procedure\n\n1. Rank and shortlist\n   Rank candidates using an explainable rubric:\n    - Input surface and reachability\n    - Boundary risk (parsing/decoding/native)\n    - Structural complexity (from targeted reading only)\n    - Existing test coverage strength and breadth\n    - Ease of harnessing\n\n### Output format\n\nFor each function/method:\n\n- qualname\n- file\n- line_range (if available)\n- score (relative)\n- rationale (2–6 bullets)\n- dependencies (key helpers, modules, external state)\n- harnessability (low/medium/high)\n\n## Approach 2: Scanning all important files yourself\n\nFor each localized Python file, read the file contents directly to extract:\n\n- Module docstring and overall structure\n- Function and async function definitions (name, args, defaults, annotations, decorators)\n- Class definitions and their methods\n- Full function bodies and implementation details\n- Docstrings for modules, classes, and functions/methods\n\nThis approach reads complete file contents, allowing for deeper analysis at the cost of higher token usage.\n\n### Procedure\n\n1. Read important files sequentially\n    - For each file from the important files list, read the full contents.\n    - Extract by direct inspection:\n        - Top-level functions and their complete implementations\n        - Classes and their methods with full bodies\n        - Nested functions if they are exposed or called by public APIs\n    - For each symbol, collect:\n        - Fully-qualified name\n        - Complete signature (from source text)\n        - Decorators\n        - Docstring (if present)\n        - Full function body\n        - Location information (file, approximate line range)\n\n2. Generate an initial candidate set using full source analysis\n   Prioritize functions/methods that:\n    - Accept bytes, str, file-like objects, dicts, or user-controlled payloads\n    - Convert between representations (raw ↔ structured)\n    - Perform validation, normalization, parsing, decoding, deserialization\n    - Touch filesystem/network/protocol boundaries\n    - Call into native extensions or bindings\n    - Contain complex control flow, loops, or recursive calls\n    - Handle exceptions or edge cases\n    - Clearly document strictness, schemas, formats, or error conditions\n\n3. Analyze implementation details from full bodies\n   For each candidate function, inspect the body for:\n    - Preconditions and assumptions (explicit checks, assertions, early returns)\n    - Internal helpers called and their purposes\n    - Error handling style and exception types raised\n    - Invariants and postconditions (explicit or implicit)\n    - Statefulness and global dependencies\n    - Input transformations and data flow\n    - Native calls or external process invocations\n    - Resource allocation and cleanup patterns\n\n4. Use tests to refine candidate selection\n    - Check if tests reference these functions/modules:\n        - Direct imports in tests\n        - Fixtures that exercise particular entry points\n        - Parameterizations over formats and inputs\n    - Down-rank candidates that are already well-covered unless they are high-risk boundaries (parsers/native bindings).\n    - Note which aspects of each function are tested vs untested.\n\n5. Rank and shortlist\n   Rank candidates using an explainable rubric:\n    - Input surface and reachability\n    - Boundary risk (parsing/decoding/native)\n    - Structural complexity (from full body analysis)\n    - Existing test coverage strength and breadth\n    - Ease of harnessing\n    - Observable implementation risks (unsafe operations, unchecked inputs, complex state)\n\n### Output format\n\nFor each function/method:\n\n- qualname\n- file\n- line_range (if available)\n- score (relative)\n- rationale (2–6 bullets)\n- dependencies (key helpers, modules, external state)\n- harnessability (low/medium/high)\n- implementation_notes (key observations from body analysis)\n\n---\n\n# Summarize existing unit tests\n\n## Goal\n\nSummarize what is already tested, infer test oracles, and identify gaps that fuzzing can complement.\n\n## Hard requirement\n\nAlways inspect and incorporate existing tests in the repository when:\n\n- Ranking functions\n- Selecting FUTs\n- Designing input models and oracles\n- Proposing seed corpus sources\n\n## Procedure\n\n1. Inventory tests\n    - Locate tests and their discovery configuration (pytest.ini, pyproject.toml, tox.ini, noxfile.py).\n    - Enumerate test modules and map them to source modules via imports.\n    - Identify shared fixtures and data factories (conftest.py, fixture files, test utilities).\n\n2. Summarize test intent\n   For each test module:\n    - What behaviors are asserted\n    - What inputs are used\n    - What exceptions are expected\n    - What invariants are implied\n\n3. Infer oracles and properties\n   Common fuzz-friendly oracles include:\n    - Round-trip properties\n    - Idempotence of normalization\n    - Parser consistency across equivalent inputs\n    - Deterministic output given deterministic input\n    - No-crash and no-hang for malformed inputs\n\n4. Identify coverage gaps\n    - FUT candidates with no direct tests\n    - Input classes not covered by tests (size extremes, malformed encodings, deep nesting, edge unicode, invalid schemas)\n    - Code paths guarded by complex conditionals or exception handlers with no tests\n\n## Output format\n\n- test_map: module_under_test → tests → asserted behaviors\n- inferred_oracles: list of reusable invariants with the functions they apply to\n- gaps: ranked list of untested or weakly-tested candidates\n\n---\n\n# Decide function under test for fuzzing\n\n## Goal\n\nSelect a final set of FUTs (typically 1–5) and produce a structured \"note to self\" for each FUT so the agent can proceed to harness implementation, corpus seeding, and fuzz execution.\n\n## Selection criteria\n\nPrefer FUTs that maximize:\n\n- Security/robustness payoff (parsing, decoding, validation, native boundary)\n- Reachability with minimal setup\n- Low existing test assurance or narrow test input coverage\n- High fuzzability (simple input channel, clear oracle or crash-only target)\n\nExplicitly weigh:\n\n- What tests already cover (and what they do not)\n- What seeds can be extracted from tests, fixtures, and sample data\n\n## Required \"note to self\" template\n\nFor each selected FUT, produce exactly this structure:\n\nFuzzing Target Note\n\n- Target:\n- File / location:\n- Why this target:\n    - Input surface:\n    - Boundary or native considerations:\n    - Complexity or path depth:\n    - Current test gaps:\n- Callable contract:\n    - Required imports or initialization:\n    - Preconditions:\n    - Determinism concerns:\n    - External dependencies:\n- Input model:\n    - Primary payload type:\n    - Decoding or parsing steps:\n    - Constraints to respect:\n    - Edge classes to emphasize:\n- Oracles:\n    - Must-hold properties:\n    - Acceptable exceptions:\n    - Suspicious exceptions:\n    - Crash-only vs correctness-checking:\n- Harness plan:\n    - Recommended approach:\n    - Minimal harness signature:\n    - Seed corpus ideas:\n    - Timeouts and resource limits:\n- Risk flags:\n    - Native extension involved:\n    - Potential DoS paths:\n    - External I/O:\n- Next actions:\n    1.\n    2.\n    3.\n    4.\n\n## Output format\n\n- selected_futs: list of chosen FUTs with brief justification\n- notes_to_self: one \"Fuzzing Target Note\" per FUT\n\n---\n\n# Final JSON block\n\nAt the end of the report, include a JSON object with:\n\n- important_files: [{path, score, rationale, indicators_hit}]\n- important_functions: [{qualname, file, line_range, score, rationale, dependencies, harnessability}]\n- test_summary: {test_map, inferred_oracles, gaps}\n- selected_futs: [{qualname, file, line_range, justification}]\n- notes_to_self: [{target_qualname, note}]\n\n\nARGUMENTS: /app/ipython"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"56e145a4-0d6b-4a75-be1f-7887ea2c2e6e","uuid":"ed540d69-79b5-4ba4-93b7-8bc00d8cb803","isSynthetic":true}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /logs/agent/sessions/skills/discover-important-function\n\n# Fuzz Target Localizer\n\n## Purpose\n\nThis skill helps an agent quickly narrow a Python repository down to a small set of high-value fuzz targets. It produces:\n\n- A ranked list of important files\n- A ranked list of important functions and methods\n- A summary of existing unit tests and inferred oracles\n- A final shortlist of functions-under-test (FUTs) for fuzzing, each with a structured \"note to self\" for follow-up actions\n\n## When to use\n\nUse this skill when the user asks to:\n\n- Find the best functions to fuzz in a Python package/library\n- Identify parsers, decoders, validators, or boundary code that is fuzz-worthy\n- Decide what to fuzz based on existing test coverage and API surface\n- Produce a structured shortlist of fuzz targets with harness guidance\n- Automating testing pipeline setup for a new or existing Python project.\n\nDo not use this skill when the user primarily wants to fix a specific bug,\nrefactor code, or implement a harness immediately\n(unless they explicitly ask for target selection first).\n\n## Inputs expected from the environment\n\nThe agent should assume access to:\n\n- Repository filesystem\n- Ability to read files\n- Ability to run local commands (optional but recommended)\n\nPreferred repository listing command:\n\n- Use `tree` to get a fast, high-signal view of repository structure (limit depth if needed).\n\nIf `tree` is not available, fall back to a recursive listing via other standard shell tooling.\n\n## Outputs\n\nProduce result:\n\nA report in any format with the following information: - Localize important files - Localize important functions - Summarize existing unit tests - Decide function under test for fuzzing\nThis file should be placed in the root of the repository as `APIs.txt`,\nwhich servers as the guidelines for future fuzzing harness implementation.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Prefer analysis and reporting over code changes.\n- Do not modify source code unless the user explicitly requests changes.\n- If running commands could be disruptive, default to read-only analysis.\n- Avoid assumptions about runtime behavior; base conclusions on code and tests.\n- Explicitly consider existing tests in the repo when selecting targets and deciding harness shape.\n- Keep the final FUT shortlist small (typically 1–5).\n\n---\n\n# Localize important files\n\n## Goal\n\nProduce a ranked list of files that are most likely to contain fuzz-worthy logic: parsing, decoding, deserialization, validation, protocol handling, file/network boundaries, or native bindings.\n\n## Procedure\n\n1. Build a repository map\n    - Start with a repository overview using `tree` to identify:\n        - Root packages and layouts (src/ layout vs flat layout)\n        - Test directories and configs\n        - Bindings/native directories\n        - Examples, docs, and tooling directories\n    - Identify packaging and metadata files such as:\n        - pyproject.toml\n        - setup.cfg\n        - setup.py\n    - Identify test configuration and entry points:\n        - tests/\n        - conftest.py\n        - pytest.ini\n        - tox.ini\n        - noxfile.py\n\n2. Exclude low-value areas\n    - Skip: virtual environments, build outputs, vendored code, documentation-only directories, examples-only directories, generated files.\n\n3. Score files using explainable heuristics\n   Assign a file a higher score when it matches more of these indicators:\n    - Public API exposure: **init**.py re-exports, **all**, api modules\n    - Input boundary keywords in file path or symbols: parse, load, dump, decode, encode, deserialize, serialize, validate, normalize, schema, protocol, message\n    - Format handlers: json, yaml, xml, csv, toml, protobuf, msgpack, pickle\n    - Regex-heavy or templating-heavy code\n    - Native boundaries: ctypes, cffi, cython, extension modules, bindings\n    - Central modules: high import fan-in across the package\n    - Test adjacency: directly imported or heavily referenced by tests\n\n4. Rank and select\n    - Produce a Top-N list (default 10–30) with a short rationale per file.\n\n## Output format\n\nFor each file:\n\n- path\n- score (relative, not necessarily normalized)\n- rationale (2–5 bullets)\n- indicators_hit (list)\n\n---\n\n# Localize important functions\n\n## Goal\n\nFrom the important files, identify and rank functions or methods that are strong fuzz targets while minimizing full-body reading until needed.\n\n## Approach 1: AST-based header and docstring scan\n\nFor each localized Python file, parse it using Python’s `ast` module and extract only:\n\n- Module docstring\n- Function and async function headers (name, args, defaults, annotations, decorators)\n- Class headers and method headers\n- Docstrings for modules, classes, and functions/methods\n\nDo not read full function bodies during the initial pass unless needed for disambiguation or final selection.\n\n### Procedure\n\n1. Build per-file declarations via AST\n    - Parse the file with `ast`\n    - Enumerate:\n        - Top-level functions\n        - Classes and their methods\n        - Nested functions only if they are likely to be directly fuzzable via an exposed wrapper\n    - For each symbol, collect:\n        - Fully-qualified name\n        - Signature details (as available from AST)\n        - Decorators\n        - Docstring (if present)\n        - Location information (file, line range if available)\n\n2. Generate an initial candidate set using headers and docstrings\n   Prioritize functions/methods that:\n    - Accept bytes, str, file-like objects, dicts, or user-controlled payloads\n    - Convert between representations (raw ↔ structured)\n    - Perform validation, normalization, parsing, decoding, deserialization\n    - Touch filesystem/network/protocol boundaries\n    - Call into native extensions or bindings\n    - Clearly document strictness, schemas, formats, or error conditions\n\n3. Use tests to refine candidate selection early\n    - Before reading full bodies, check if tests reference these functions/modules:\n        - Direct imports in tests\n        - Fixtures that exercise particular entry points\n        - Parameterizations over formats and inputs\n    - Down-rank candidates that are already well-covered unless they are high-risk boundaries (parsers/native bindings).\n\n4. Confirm with targeted reading only for top candidates\n   For the top candidates (typically 10–20), read the full function bodies and capture:\n    - Preconditions and assumptions\n    - Internal helpers called\n    - Error handling style and exception types\n    - Any obvious invariants and postconditions\n    - Statefulness and global dependencies\n\n5. Rank and shortlist\n   Rank candidates using an explainable rubric:\n    - Input surface and reachability\n    - Boundary risk (parsing/decoding/native)\n    - Structural complexity (from targeted reading only)\n    - Existing test coverage strength and breadth\n    - Ease of harnessing\n\n### Output format\n\nFor each function/method:\n\n- qualname\n- file\n- line_range (if available)\n- score (relative)\n- rationale (2–6 bullets)\n- dependencies (key helpers, modules, external state)\n- harnessability (low/medium/high)\n\n## Approach 2: Scanning all important files yourself\n\nFor each localized Python file, read the file contents directly to extract:\n\n- Module docstring and overall structure\n- Function and async function definitions (name, args, defaults, annotations, decorators)\n- Class definitions and their methods\n- Full function bodies and implementation details\n- Docstrings for modules, classes, and functions/methods\n\nThis approach reads complete file contents, allowing for deeper analysis at the cost of higher token usage.\n\n### Procedure\n\n1. Use tests to refine candidate selection early\n    - Before reading full bodies, check if tests reference these functions/modules:\n        - Direct imports in tests\n        - Fixtures that exercise particular entry points\n        - Parameterizations over formats and inputs\n    - Down-rank candidates that are already well-covered unless they are high-risk boundaries (parsers/native bindings).\n\n4. Confirm with targeted reading only for top candidates\n   For the top candidates (typically 10–20), read the full function bodies and capture:\n    - Preconditions and assumptions\n    - Internal helpers called\n    - Error handling style and exception types\n    - Any obvious invariants and postconditions\n    - Statefulness and global dependencies\n\n5. Rank and shortlist\n   Rank candidates using an explainable rubric:\n    - Input surface and reachability\n    - Boundary risk (parsing/decoding/native)\n    - Structural complexity (from targeted reading only)\n    - Existing test coverage strength and breadth\n    - Ease of harnessing\n\n### Output format\n\nFor each function/method:\n\n- qualname\n- file\n- line_range (if available)\n- score (relative)\n- rationale (2–6 bullets)\n- dependencies (key helpers, modules, external state)\n- harnessability (low/medium/high)\n\n## Approach 2: Scanning all important files yourself\n\nFor each localized Python file, read the file contents directly to extract:\n\n- Module docstring and overall structure\n- Function and async function definitions (name, args, defaults, annotations, decorators)\n- Class definitions and their methods\n- Full function bodies and implementation details\n- Docstrings for modules, classes, and functions/methods\n\nThis approach reads complete file contents, allowing for deeper analysis at the cost of higher token usage.\n\n### Procedure\n\n1. Read important files sequentially\n    - For each file from the important files list, read the full contents.\n    - Extract by direct inspection:\n        - Top-level functions and their complete implementations\n        - Classes and their methods with full bodies\n        - Nested functions if they are exposed or called by public APIs\n    - For each symbol, collect:\n        - Fully-qualified name\n        - Complete signature (from source text)\n        - Decorators\n        - Docstring (if present)\n        - Full function body\n        - Location information (file, approximate line range)\n\n2. Generate an initial candidate set using full source analysis\n   Prioritize functions/methods that:\n    - Accept bytes, str, file-like objects, dicts, or user-controlled payloads\n    - Convert between representations (raw ↔ structured)\n    - Perform validation, normalization, parsing, decoding, deserialization\n    - Touch filesystem/network/protocol boundaries\n    - Call into native extensions or bindings\n    - Contain complex control flow, loops, or recursive calls\n    - Handle exceptions or edge cases\n    - Clearly document strictness, schemas, formats, or error conditions\n\n3. Analyze implementation details from full bodies\n   For each candidate function, inspect the body for:\n    - Preconditions and assumptions (explicit checks, assertions, early returns)\n    - Internal helpers called and their purposes\n    - Error handling style and exception types raised\n    - Invariants and postconditions (explicit or implicit)\n    - Statefulness and global dependencies\n    - Input transformations and data flow\n    - Native calls or external process invocations\n    - Resource allocation and cleanup patterns\n\n4. Use tests to refine candidate selection\n    - Check if tests reference these functions/modules:\n        - Direct imports in tests\n        - Fixtures that exercise particular entry points\n        - Parameterizations over formats and inputs\n    - Down-rank candidates that are already well-covered unless they are high-risk boundaries (parsers/native bindings).\n    - Note which aspects of each function are tested vs untested.\n\n5. Rank and shortlist\n   Rank candidates using an explainable rubric:\n    - Input surface and reachability\n    - Boundary risk (parsing/decoding/native)\n    - Structural complexity (from full body analysis)\n    - Existing test coverage strength and breadth\n    - Ease of harnessing\n    - Observable implementation risks (unsafe operations, unchecked inputs, complex state)\n\n### Output format\n\nFor each function/method:\n\n- qualname\n- file\n- line_range (if available)\n- score (relative)\n- rationale (2–6 bullets)\n- dependencies (key helpers, modules, external state)\n- harnessability (low/medium/high)\n- implementation_notes (key observations from body analysis)\n\n---\n\n# Summarize existing unit tests\n\n## Goal\n\nSummarize what is already tested, infer test oracles, and identify gaps that fuzzing can complement.\n\n## Hard requirement\n\nAlways inspect and incorporate existing tests in the repository when:\n\n- Ranking functions\n- Selecting FUTs\n- Designing input models and oracles\n- Proposing seed corpus sources\n\n## Procedure\n\n1. Inventory tests\n    - Locate tests and their discovery configuration (pytest.ini, pyproject.toml, tox.ini, noxfile.py).\n    - Enumerate test modules and map them to source modules via imports.\n    - Identify shared fixtures and data factories (conftest.py, fixture files, test utilities).\n\n2. Infer oracles and properties\n   Common fuzz-friendly oracles include:\n    - Round-trip properties\n    - Idempotence of normalization\n    - Parser consistency across equivalent inputs\n    - Deterministic output given deterministic input\n    - No-crash and no-hang for malformed inputs\n\n4. Identify coverage gaps\n    - FUT candidates with no direct tests\n    - Input classes not covered by tests (size extremes, malformed encodings, deep nesting, edge unicode, invalid schemas)\n    - Code paths guarded by complex conditionals or exception handlers with no tests\n\n## Output format\n\n- test_map: module_under_test → tests → asserted behaviors\n- inferred_oracles: list of reusable invariants with the functions they apply to\n- gaps: ranked list of untested or weakly-tested candidates\n\n---\n\n# Decide function under test for fuzzing\n\n## Goal\n\nSelect a final set of FUTs (typically 1–5) and produce a structured \"note to self\" for each FUT so the agent can proceed to harness implementation, corpus seeding, and fuzz execution.\n\n## Selection criteria\n\nPrefer FUTs that maximize:\n\n- Security/robustness payoff (parsing, decoding, validation, native boundary)\n- Reachability with minimal setup\n- Low existing test assurance or narrow test input coverage\n- High fuzzability (simple input channel, clear oracle or crash-only target)\n\nExplicitly weigh:\n\n- What tests already cover (and what they do not)\n- What seeds can be extracted from tests, fixtures, and sample data\n\n## Required \"note to self\" template\n\nFor each selected FUT, produce exactly this structure:\n\nFuzzing Target Note\n\n- Target:\n- File / location:\n- Why this target:\n    - {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_vrtx_01Mnv3xfKeStrgVDobetcqxJ","type":"tool_result","content":"     1→Arrow: Better dates & times for Python\n     2→======================================\n     3→\n     4→.. start-inclusion-marker-do-not-remove\n     5→\n     6→.. image:: https://github.com/arrow-py/arrow/workflows/tests/badge.svg?branch=master\n     7→   :alt: Build Status\n     8→   :target: https://github.com/arrow-py/arrow/actions?query=workflow%3Atests+branch%3Amaster\n     9→\n    10→.. image:: https://codecov.io/gh/arrow-py/arrow/branch/master/graph/badge.svg\n    11→   :alt: Coverage\n    12→   :target: https://codecov.io/gh/arrow-py/arrow\n    13→\n    14→.. image:: https://img.shields.io/pypi/v/arrow.svg\n    15→   :alt: PyPI Version\n    16→   :target: https://pypi.python.org/pypi/arrow\n    17→\n    18→.. image:: https://img.shields.io/pypi/pyversions/arrow.svg\n    19→   :alt: Supported Python Versions\n    20→   :target: https://pypi.python.org/pypi/arrow\n    21→\n    22→.. image:: https://img.shields.io/pypi/l/arrow.svg\n    23→   :alt: License\n    24→   :target: https://pypi.python.org/pypi/arrow\n    25→\n    26→.. image:: https://img.shields.io/badge/code%20style-black-000000.svg\n    27→   :alt: Code Style: Black\n    28→   :target: https://github.com/psf/black\n    29→\n    30→\n    31→**Arrow** is a Python library that offers a sensible and human-friendly approach to creating, manipulating, formatting and converting dates, times and timestamps. It implements and updates the datetime type, plugging gaps in functionality and providing an intelligent module API that supports many common creation scenarios. Simply put, it helps you work with dates and times with fewer imports and a lot less code.\n    32→\n    33→Arrow is named after the `arrow of time <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrow_of_time>`_ and is heavily inspired by `moment.js <https://github.com/moment/moment>`_ and `requests <https://github.com/psf/requests>`_.\n    34→\n    35→Why use Arrow over built-in modules?\n    36→------------------------------------\n    37→\n    38→Python's standard library and some other low-level modules have near-complete date, time and timezone functionality, but don't work very well from a usability perspective:\n    39→\n    40→- Too many modules: datetime, time, calendar, dateutil, pytz and more\n    41→- Too many types: date, time, datetime, tzinfo, timedelta, relativedelta, etc.\n    42→- Timezones and timestamp conversions are verbose and unpleasant\n    43→- Timezone naivety is the norm\n    44→- Gaps in functionality: ISO 8601 parsing, timespans, humanization\n    45→\n    46→Features\n    47→--------\n    48→\n    49→- Fully-implemented, drop-in replacement for datetime\n    50→- Support for Python 3.8+\n    51→- Timezone-aware and UTC by default\n    52→- Super-simple creation options for many common input scenarios\n    53→- ``shift`` method with support for relative offsets, including weeks\n    54→- Format and parse strings automatically\n    55→- Wide support for the `ISO 8601 <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISO_8601>`_ standard\n    56→- Timezone conversion\n    57→- Support for ``dateutil``, ``pytz``, and ``ZoneInfo`` tzinfo objects\n    58→- Generates time spans, ranges, floors and ceilings for time frames ranging from microsecond to year\n    59→- Humanize dates and times with a growing list of contributed locales\n    60→- Extensible for your own Arrow-derived types\n    61→- Full support for PEP 484-style type hints\n    62→\n    63→Quick Start\n    64→-----------\n    65→\n    66→Installation\n    67→~~~~~~~~~~~~\n    68→\n It implements and updates the datetime type, plugging gaps in functionality and providing an intelligent module API that supports many common creation scenarios. Simply put, it helps you work with dates and times with fewer imports and a lot less code.\n\nArrow is named after the `arrow of time <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrow_of_time>`_ and is heavily inspired by `moment.js <https://github.com/moment/moment>`_ and `requests <https://github.com/psf/requests>`_.\n\nWhy use Arrow over built-in modules?\n------------------------------------\n\nPython's standard library and some other low-level modules have near-complete date, time and timezone functionality, but don't work very well from a usability perspective:\n\n- Too many modules: datetime, time, calendar, dateutil, pytz and more\n- Too many types: date, time, datetime, tzinfo, timedelta, relativedelta, etc.\n- Timezones and timestamp conversions are verbose and unpleasant\n- Timezone naivety is the norm\n- Gaps in functionality: ISO 8601 parsing, timespans, humanization\n\nFeatures\n--------\n\n- Fully-implemented, drop-in replacement for datetime\n- Support for Python 3.8+\n- Timezone-aware and UTC by default\n- Super-simple creation options for many common input scenarios\n- ``shift`` method with support for relative offsets, including weeks\n- Format and parse strings automatically\n- Wide support for the `ISO 8601 <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISO_8601>`_ standard\n- Timezone conversion\n- Support for ``dateutil``, ``pytz``, and ``ZoneInfo`` tzinfo objects\n- Generates time spans, ranges, floors and ceilings for time frames ranging from microsecond to year\n- Humanize dates and times with a growing list of contributed locales\n- Extensible for your own Arrow-derived types\n- Full support for PEP 484-style type hints\n\nQuick Start\n-----------\n\nInstallation\n~~~~~~~~~~~~\n\nTo install Arrow, use `pip <https://pip.pypa.io/en/stable/quickstart/>`_ or `pipenv <https://docs.pipenv.org>`_:\n\n.. code-block:: console\n\n    $ pip install -U arrow\n\nExample Usage\n~~~~~~~~~~~~~\n\n.. code-block:: python\n\n    >>> import arrow\n    >>> arrow.get('2013-05-11T21:23:58.970460+07:00')\n    <Arrow [2013-05-11T21:23:58.970460+07:00]>\n\n    >>> utc = arrow.utcnow()\n    >>> utc\n    <Arrow [2013-05-11T21:23:58.970460+00:00]>\n\n    >>> utc = utc.shift(hours=-1)\n    >>> utc\n    <Arrow [2013-05-11T20:23:58.970460+00:00]>\n\n    >>> local = utc.to('US/Pacific')\n    >>> local\n    <Arrow [2013-05-11T13:23:58.970460-07:00]>\n\n    >>> local.timestamp()\n    1368303838.970460\n\n    >>> local.format()\n    '2013-05-11 13:23:58 -07:00'\n\n    >>> local.format('YYYY-MM-DD HH:mm:ss ZZ')\n    '2013-05-11 13:23:58 -07:00'\n\n    >>> local.humanize()\n    'an hour ago'\n\n    >>> local.humanize(locale='ko-kr')\n    '한시간 전'\n\n.. end-inclusion-marker-do-not-remove\n\nDocumentation\n-------------\n\nFor full documentation, please visit `arrow.readthedocs.io <https://arrow.readthedocs.io>`_.\n\nContributing\n------------\n\nContributions are welcome for both code and localizations (adding and updating locales). Begin by gaining familiarity with the Arrow library and its features. Then, jump into contributing:\n\n#. Find an issue or feature to tackle on the `issue tracker <https://github.com/arrow-py/arrow/issues>`_. Issues marked with the `\"good first issue\" label <https://github.com/arrow-py/arrow/issues?q=is%3Aopen+is%3Aissue+label%3A%22good+first+issue%22>`_ may be a great place to start!\n#. Fork `this repository <https://github.com/arrow-py/arrow>`_ on GitHub and begin making changes in a branch.\n#. Add a few tests to ensure that the bug was fixed or the feature works as expected.\n#. Submit a pull request and await feedback 😃.\n\nIf you have any questions along the way, feel free to ask them `here <https://github.com/arrow-py/arrow/discussions>`_.\n\nSupport Arrow\n-------------\n\n`Open Collective <https://opencollective.com/>`_ is an online funding platform that provides tools to raise money and share your finances with full transparency. It is the platform of choice for individuals and companies to make one-time or recurring donations directly to the project. If you are interested in making a financial contribution, please visit the `Arrow collective <https://opencollective.com/arrow>`_.\n","numLines":135,"startLine":1,"totalLines":135}}}

By using it, you agree to cede\n    20→control over minutiae of hand-formatting. In return, _Black_ gives you speed,\n    21→determinism, and freedom from `pycodestyle` nagging about formatting. You will save time\n    22→and mental energy for more important matters.\n    23→\n    24→Blackened code looks the same regardless of the project you're reading. Formatting\n    25→becomes transparent after a while and you can focus on the content instead.\n    26→\n    27→_Black_ makes code review faster by producing the smallest diffs possible.\n    28→\n    29→Try it out now using the [Black Playground](https://black.vercel.app). Watch the\n    30→[PyCon 2019 talk](https://youtu.be/esZLCuWs_2Y) to learn more.\n    31→\n    32→---\n    33→\n    34→**[Read the documentation on ReadTheDocs!](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable)**\n    35→\n    36→---\n    37→\n    38→## Installation and usage\n    39→\n    40→### Installation\n    41→\n    42→_Black_ can be installed by running `pip install black`. It requires Python 3.10+ to\n    43→run. If you want to format Jupyter Notebooks, install with\n    44→`pip install \"black[jupyter]\"`.\n    45→\n    46→### Usage\n    47→\n    48→To get started right away with sensible defaults:\n    49→\n    50→```sh\n    51→black {source_file_or_directory}\n    52→```\n    53→\n    54→You can run _Black_ as a package if running it as a script doesn't work:\n    55→\n    56→```sh\n    57→python -m black {source_file_or_directory}\n    58→```\n    59→\n    60→Further information can be found in our docs:\n    61→\n    62→- [Usage and Configuration](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/usage_and_configuration/index.html)\n    63→\n    64→_Black_ is already [successfully used](https://github.com/psf/black#used-by) by many\n    65→projects, small and big. _Black_ has a comprehensive test suite, with efficient parallel\n    66→tests, and our own auto formatting and parallel Continuous Integration runner. Now that\n    67→we have become stable, you should not expect large formatting changes in the future.\n    68→Stylistic changes will mostly be responses to bug reports and support for new Python\n    69→syntax. For more information please refer to\n    70→[The Black Code Style](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/the_black_code_style/index.html).\n    71→\n    72→Also, as a safety measure which slows down processing, _Black_ will check that the\n    73→reformatted code still produces a valid AST that is effectively equivalent to the\n    74→original (see the\n    75→[Pragmatism](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/the_black_code_style/current_style.html#ast-before-and-after-formatting)\n    76→section for details). If you're feeling confident, use `--fast`.\n    77→\n    78→## The _Black_ code style\n    79→\n    80→_Black_ is a PEP 8 compliant opinionated formatter. _Black_ reformats entire files in\n    81→place. Style configuration options are deliberately limited and rarely added. It doesn't\n    82→take previous formatting into account (see\n    83→[Pragmatism](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/the_black_code_style/current_style.html#pragmatism)\n    84→for exceptions).\n    85→\n    86→Our documentation covers the current _Black_ code style, but planned changes to it are\n    87→also documented. They're both worth taking a look at:\n    88→\n    89→- [The _Black_ Code Style: Current style](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/the_black_code_style/current_style.html)\n    90→- [The _Black_ Code Style: Future style](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/the_black_code_style/future_style.html)\n    91→\n    92→Changes to the _Black_ code style are bound by the Stability Policy:\n    93→\n    94→- [The _Black_ Code Style: Stability Policy](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/the_black_code_style/index.html#stability-policy)\n    95→\n    96→Please refer to this document before submitting an issue. What seems like a bug might be\n    97→intended behaviour.\n    98→\n    99→### Pragmatism\n   100→\n   101→Early versions of _Black_ used to be absolutist in some respects. They took after its\n   102→initial author. This was fine at the time as it made the implementation simpler and\n   103→there were not many users anyway. Not many edge cases were reported. As a mature tool,\n   104→_Black_ does make some exceptions to rules it otherwise holds.\n   105→\n   106→- [The _Black_ code style: Pragmatism](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/the_black_code_style/current_style.html#pragmatism)\n   107→\n   108→Please refer to this document before submitting an issue just like with the document\n   109→above. What seems like a bug might be intended behaviour.\n   110→\n   111→## Configuration\n   112→\n   113→_Black_ is able to read project-specific default values for its command line options\n   114→from a `pyproject.toml` file. This is especially useful for specifying custom\n   115→`--include` and `--exclude`/`--force-exclude`/`--extend-exclude` patterns for your\n   116→project.\n   117→\n   118→You can find more details in our documentation:\n   119→\n   120→- [The basics: Configuration via a file](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/usage_and_configuration/the_basics.html#configuration-via-a-file)\n   121→\n   122→And if you're looking for more general configuration documentation:\n   123→\n   124→- [Usage and Configuration](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/usage_and_configuration/index.html)\n   125→\n   126→**Pro-tip**: If you're asking yourself \"Do I need to configure anything?\" the answer is\n   127→\"No\". _Black_ is all about sensible defaults. Applying those defaults will have your\n   128→code in compliance with many other _Black_ formatted projects.\n   129→\n   130→## Used by\n   131→\n   132→The following notable open-source projects trust _Black_ with enforcing a consistent\n   133→code style: pytest, tox, Pyramid, Django, Django Channels, Hypothesis, attrs,\n   134→SQLAlchemy, Poetry, PyPA applications (Warehouse, Bandersnatch, Pipenv, virtualenv),\n   135→pandas, Pillow, Twisted, LocalStack, every Datadog Agent Integration, Home Assistant,\n   136→Zulip, Kedro, OpenOA, FLORIS, ORBIT, WOMBAT, and many more.\n   137→\n   138→The following organizations use _Black_: Dropbox, KeepTruckin, Lyft, Mozilla, Quora,\n   139→Duolingo, QuantumBlack, Tesla, Archer Aviation.\n   140→\n   141→Are we missing anyone? I can now do refactorings in about\n   149→> 1% of the keystrokes that it would have taken me previously when we had no way for\n   150→> code to format itself.\n   151→\n   152→**Dusty Phillips**,\n   153→[writer](https://smile.amazon.com/s/ref=nb_sb_noss?url=search-alias%3Daps&field-keywords=dusty+phillips):\n   154→\n   155→> _Black_ is opinionated so you don't have to be.\n   156→\n   157→**Hynek Schlawack**, [creator of `attrs`](https://www.attrs.org/), core developer of\n   158→Twisted and CPython:\n   159→\n   160→> An auto-formatter that doesn't suck is all I want for Xmas!\n   161→\n   162→**Carl Meyer**, [Django](https://www.djangoproject.com/) core developer:\n   163→\n   164→> At least the name is good.\n   165→\n   166→**Kenneth Reitz**, creator of [`requests`](https://requests.readthedocs.io/en/latest/)\n   167→and [`pipenv`](https://readthedocs.org/projects/pipenv/):\n   168→\n   169→> This vastly improves the formatting of our code. Thanks a ton!\n   170→\n   171→## Show your style\n   172→\n   173→Use the badge in your project's README.md:\n   174→\n   175→```md\n   176→[![Code style: black](https://img.shields.io/badge/code%20style-black-000000.svg)](https://github.com/psf/black)\n   177→```\n   178→\n   179→Using the badge in README.rst:\n   180→\n   181→```rst\n   182→.. image:: https://img.shields.io/badge/code%20style-black-000000.svg\n   183→    :target: https://github.com/psf/black\n   184→```\n   185→\n   186→Looks like this:\n   187→[![Code style: black](https://img.shields.io/badge/code%20style-black-000000.svg)](https://github.com/psf/black)\n   188→\n   189→## License\n   190→\n   191→MIT\n   192→\n   193→## Contributing\n   194→\n   195→Welcome! By using it, you agree to cede\ncontrol over minutiae of hand-formatting. In return, _Black_ gives you speed,\ndeterminism, and freedom from `pycodestyle` nagging about formatting. You will save time\nand mental energy for more important matters.\n\nBlackened code looks the same regardless of the project you're reading. Formatting\nbecomes transparent after a while and you can focus on the content instead.\n\n_Black_ makes code review faster by producing the smallest diffs possible.\n\nTry it out now using the [Black Playground](https://black.vercel.app). Watch the\n[PyCon 2019 talk](https://youtu.be/esZLCuWs_2Y) to learn more.\n\n---\n\n**[Read the documentation on ReadTheDocs!](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable)**\n\n---\n\n## Installation and usage\n\n### Installation\n\n_Black_ can be installed by running `pip install black`. It requires Python 3.10+ to\nrun. If you want to format Jupyter Notebooks, install with\n`pip install \"black[jupyter]\"`.\n\n### Usage\n\nTo get started right away with sensible defaults:\n\n```sh\nblack {source_file_or_directory}\n```\n\nYou can run _Black_ as a package if running it as a script doesn't work:\n\n```sh\npython -m black {source_file_or_directory}\n```\n\nFurther information can be found in our docs:\n\n- [Usage and Configuration](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/usage_and_configuration/index.html)\n\n_Black_ is already [successfully used](https://github.com/psf/black#used-by) by many\nprojects, small and big. _Black_ has a comprehensive test suite, with efficient parallel\ntests, and our own auto formatting and parallel Continuous Integration runner. Now that\nwe have become stable, you should not expect large formatting changes in the future.\nStylistic changes will mostly be responses to bug reports and support for new Python\nsyntax. For more information please refer to\n[The Black Code Style](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/the_black_code_style/index.html).\n\nAlso, as a safety measure which slows down processing, _Black_ will check that the\nreformatted code still produces a valid AST that is effectively equivalent to the\noriginal (see the\n[Pragmatism](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/the_black_code_style/current_style.html#ast-before-and-after-formatting)\nsection for details). If you're feeling confident, use `--fast`.\n\n## The _Black_ code style\n\n_Black_ is a PEP 8 compliant opinionated formatter. _Black_ reformats entire files in\nplace. Style configuration options are deliberately limited and rarely added. It doesn't\ntake previous formatting into account (see\n[Pragmatism](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/the_black_code_style/current_style.html#pragmatism)\nfor exceptions).\n\nOur documentation covers the current _Black_ code style, but planned changes to it are\nalso documented. They're both worth taking a look at:\n\n- [The _Black_ Code Style: Current style](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/the_black_code_style/current_style.html)\n- [The _Black_ Code Style: Future style](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/the_black_code_style/future_style.html)\n\nChanges to the _Black_ code style are bound by the Stability Policy:\n\n- [The _Black_ Code Style: Stability Policy](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/the_black_code_style/index.html#stability-policy)\n\nPlease refer to this document before submitting an issue. What seems like a bug might be\nintended behaviour.\n\n### Pragmatism\n\nEarly versions of _Black_ used to be absolutist in some respects. They took after its\ninitial author. This was fine at the time as it made the implementation simpler and\nthere were not many users anyway. Not many edge cases were reported. As a mature tool,\n_Black_ does make some exceptions to rules it otherwise holds.\n\n- [The _Black_ code style: Pragmatism](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/the_black_code_style/current_style.html#pragmatism)\n\nPlease refer to this document before submitting an issue just like with the document\nabove. What seems like a bug might be intended behaviour.\n\n## Configuration\n\n_Black_ is able to read project-specific default values for its command line options\nfrom a `pyproject.toml` file. This is especially useful for specifying custom\n`--include` and `--exclude`/`--force-exclude`/`--extend-exclude` patterns for your\nproject.\n\nYou can find more details in our documentation:\n\n- [The basics: Configuration via a file](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/usage_and_configuration/the_basics.html#configuration-via-a-file)\n\nAnd if you're looking for more general configuration documentation:\n\n- [Usage and Configuration](https://black.readthedocs.io/en/stable/usage_and_configuration/index.html)\n\n**Pro-tip**: If you're asking yourself \"Do I need to configure anything?\" the answer is\n\"No\". _Black_ is all about sensible defaults. Applying those defaults will have your\ncode in compliance with many other _Black_ formatted projects.\n\n## Used by\n\nThe following notable open-source projects trust _Black_ with enforcing a consistent\ncode style: pytest, tox, Pyramid, Django, Django Channels, Hypothesis, attrs,\nSQLAlchemy, Poetry, PyPA applications (Warehouse, Bandersnatch, Pipenv, virtualenv),\npandas, Pillow, Twisted, LocalStack, every Datadog Agent Integration, Home Assistant,\nZulip, Kedro, OpenOA, FLORIS, ORBIT, WOMBAT, and many more.\n\nThe following organizations use _Black_: Dropbox, KeepTruckin, Lyft, Mozilla, Quora,\nDuolingo, QuantumBlack, Tesla, Archer Aviation.\n\nAre we missing anyone? Let us know.\n\n## Testimonials\n\n**Mike Bayer**, [author of `SQLAlchemy`](https://www.sqlalchemy.org/):\n\n> I can't think of any single tool in my entire programming career that has given me a\n> bigger productivity increase by its introduction. I can now do refactorings in about\n> 1% of the keystrokes that it would have taken me previously when we had no way for\n> code to format itself.\n\n**Dusty Phillips**,\n[writer](https://smile.amazon.com/s/ref=nb_sb_noss?url=search-alias%3Daps&field-keywords=dusty+phillips):\n\n> _Black_ is opinionated so you don't have to be.\n\n**Hynek Schlawack**, [creator of `attrs`](https://www.attrs.org/), core developer of\nTwisted and CPython:\n\n> An auto-formatter that doesn't suck is all I want for Xmas!\n\n**Carl Meyer**, [Django](https://www.djangoproject.com/) core developer:\n\n> At least the name is good.\n\n**Kenneth Reitz**, creator of [`requests`](https://requests.readthedocs.io/en/latest/)\nand [`pipenv`](https://readthedocs.org/projects/pipenv/):\n\n> This vastly improves the formatting of our code. As a result, this library\n> has been put into a *maintenance-only* mode. Support for new Python versions\n> will be added and critical bugs and security issues will still be\n> fixed but all other changes will be rejected. Users are encouraged to migrate\n> to [orjson](https://pypi.org/project/orjson/) which is both much faster and\n> less likely to introduce a surprise buffer overflow vulnerability in the\n> future.\n\n## Usage\n\nMay be used as a drop in replacement for most other JSON parsers for Python:\n\n```pycon\n>>> import ujson\n>>> ujson.dumps([{\"key\": \"value\"}, 81, True])\n'[{\"key\":\"value\"},81,true]'\n>>> ujson.loads(\"\"\"[{\"key\": \"value\"}, 81, true]\"\"\")\n[{'key': 'value'}, 81, True]\n```\n\n### Encoder options\n\n#### encode_html_chars\n\nUsed to enable special encoding of \"unsafe\" HTML characters into safer Unicode\nsequences. Default is `False`:\n\n```pycon\n>>> ujson.dumps(\"<script>John&Doe\", encode_html_chars=True)\n'\"\\\\u003cscript\\\\u003eJohn\\\\u0026Doe\"'\n```\n\n#### ensure_ascii\n\nLimits output to ASCII and escapes all extended characters above 127. Default is `True`.\nIf your end format supports UTF-8, setting this option to false is highly recommended to\nsave space:\n\n```pycon\n>>> ujson.dumps(\"åäö\")\n'\"\\\\u00e5\\\\u00e4\\\\u00f6\"'\n>>> ujson.dumps(\"åäö\", ensure_ascii=False)\n'\"åäö\"'\n```\n\n#### escape_forward_slashes\n\nControls whether forward slashes (`/`) are escaped. Default is `True`:\n\n```pycon\n>>> ujson.dumps(\"https://example.com\")\n'\"https:\\\\/\\\\/example.com\"'\n>>> ujson.dumps(\"https://example.com\", escape_forward_slashes=False)\n'\"https://example.com\"'\n```\n\n#### indent\n\nControls whether indentation (\"pretty output\") is enabled. Default is `0` (disabled):\n\n```pycon\n>>> ujson.dumps({\"foo\": \"bar\"})\n'{\"foo\":\"bar\"}'\n>>> print(ujson.dumps({\"foo\": \"bar\"}, indent=4))\n{\n    \"foo\": \"bar\"\n}\n```\n\n## With a compact codebase of **~5,000 lines of Python**, it serves as both a capable inference engine and a transparent reference for researchers and developers.\n\n## ✨ Key Features\n\n- **High Performance**: Achieves state-of-the-art throughput and latency with advanced optimizations.\n- **Lightweight & Readable**: A clean, modular, and fully type-annotated codebase that is easy to understand and modify.\n- **Advanced Optimizations**:\n  - **Radix Cache**: Reuses KV cache for shared prefixes across requests.\n  - **Chunked Prefill**: Reduces peak memory usage for long-context serving.\n  - **Overlap Scheduling**: Hides CPU scheduling overhead with GPU computation.\n  - **Tensor Parallelism**: Scales inference across multiple GPUs.\n  - **Optimized Kernels**: Integrates **FlashAttention** and **FlashInfer** for maximum efficiency.\n  - ...\n\n## 🚀 Quick Start\n\n> **⚠️ Platform Support**: Mini-SGLang currently supports **Linux only** (x86_64 and aarch64). Windows and macOS are not supported due to dependencies on Linux-specific CUDA kernels (`sgl-kernel`, `flashinfer`). We recommend using [WSL2](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/wsl/install) on Windows or Docker for cross-platform compatibility.\n\n### 1. Environment Setup\n\nWe recommend using `uv` for a fast and reliable installation (note that `uv` does not conflict with `conda`).\n\n```bash\n# Create a virtual environment (Python 3.10+ recommended)\nuv venv --python=3.12\nsource .venv/bin/activate\n```\n\n**Prerequisites**: Mini-SGLang relies on CUDA kernels that are JIT-compiled. Ensure you have the **NVIDIA CUDA Toolkit** installed and that its version matches your driver's version. You can check your driver's CUDA capability with `nvidia-smi`.\n\n### 2. Installation\n\nInstall Mini-SGLang directly from the source:\n\n```bash\ngit clone https://github.com/sgl-project/mini-sglang.git\ncd mini-sglang && uv venv --python=3.12 && source .venv/bin/activate\nuv pip install -e .\n```\n\n<details>\n<summary><b>💡 Installing on Windows (WSL2)</b></summary>\n\nSince Mini-SGLang requires Linux-specific dependencies, Windows users should use WSL2:\n\n1. **Install WSL2** (if not already installed):\n   ```powershell\n   # In PowerShell (as Administrator)\n   wsl --install\n   ```\n\n2. **Install CUDA on WSL2**:\n   - Follow [NVIDIA's WSL2 CUDA guide](https://docs.nvidia.com/cuda/wsl-user-guide/index.html)\n   - Ensure your Windows GPU drivers support WSL2\n\n3. **Install Mini-SGLang in WSL2**:\n   ```bash\n   # Inside WSL2 terminal\n   git clone https://github.com/sgl-project/mini-sglang.git\n   cd mini-sglang && uv venv --python=3.12 && source .venv/bin/activate\n   uv pip install -e .\n   ```\n\n4. **Access from Windows**: The server will be accessible at `http://localhost:8000` from Windows browsers and applications.\n\n</details>\n\n### 3. Online Serving\n\nLaunch an OpenAI-compatible API server with a single command.\n\n```bash\n# Deploy Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B on a single GPU\npython -m minisgl --model \"Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B\"\n\n# Deploy meta-llama/Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct on 4 GPUs with Tensor Parallelism, on port 30000\npython -m minisgl --model \"meta-llama/Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct\" --tp 4 --port 30000\n```\n\nOnce the server is running, you can send requests using standard tools like `curl` or any OpenAI-compatible client.\n\n### 4. Interactive Shell\n\nChat with your model directly in the terminal by adding the `--shell` flag.\n\n```bash\npython -m minisgl --model \"Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B\" --shell\n```\n\n![shell-example](https://lmsys.org/images/blog/minisgl/shell.png)\n\nYou can also use `/reset` to clear the chat history.\n\n## Benchmark\n\n### Offline inference\n\nSee [bench.py](./benchmark/offline/bench.py) for more details. Set `MINISGL_DISABLE_OVERLAP_SCHEDULING=1` for ablation study on overlap scheduling.\n\nTest Configuration:\n\n- Hardware: 1xH200 GPU.\n- Model: Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-14B\n- Total Requests: 256 sequences\n- Input Length: Randomly sampled between 100-1024 tokens\n- Output Length: Randomly sampled between 100-1024 tokens\n\n![offline](https://lmsys.org/images/blog/minisgl/offline.png)\n\n### Online inference\n\nSee [benchmark_qwen.py](./benchmark/online/bench_qwen.py) for more details.\n\nTest Configuration:\n\n- Hardware: 4xH200 GPU, connected by NVLink.\n- Model: Qwen3-32B\n- Dataset: [Qwen trace](https://github.com/alibaba-edu/qwen-bailian-usagetraces-anon/blob/main/qwen_traceA_blksz_16.jsonl), replaying first 1000 requests.\n\nLaunch command:\n\n```bash\n# Mini-SGLang\npython -m minisgl --model \"Qwen/Qwen3-32B\" --tp 4 --cache naive\n\n# SGLang\npython3 -m sglang.launch_server --model \"Qwen/Qwen3-32B\" --tp 4 \\\n    --disable-radix --port 1919 --decode-attention flashinfer\n```\n\n![online](https://lmsys.org/images/blog/minisgl/online.png)\n\n## 📚 Learn More\n\n- **[Detailed Features](./docs/features.md)**: Explore all available features and command-line arguments.\n- **[System Architecture](./docs/structures.md)**: Dive deep into the design and data flow of Mini-SGLang.\n","numLines":147,"startLine":1,"totalLines":147}}}
